=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Given a string s and an integer k, return the total number of substrings of s where at least one character appears at least k times.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "abacb", k = 2
Output: 4
Explanation:
The valid substrings are:

"aba" (character 'a' appears 2 times).
"abac" (character 'a' appears 2 times).
"abacb" (character 'a' appears 2 times).
"bacb" (character 'b' appears 2 times).


Example 2:

Input: s = "abcde", k = 1
Output: 15
Explanation:
All substrings are valid because every character appears at least once.

 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 3000
1 <= k <= s.length
s consists only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        ans = 0
        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                cnt[ord(s[right]) - 97] += 1
                if cnt[ord(s[right]) - 97] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break
        return ans

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        ans = 0
        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                idx = ord(s[right]) - 97
                cnt[idx] += 1
                if cnt[idx] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break
        return ans

This is fine.

Potential micro-optimization: Instead of `ord(s[right]) - 97` each time, could precompute `arr = [ord(c)-97 for c in s]` and use `arr[right]`. That may be faster. Code:

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        arr = [ord(c) - 97 for c in s]
        ans = 0
        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                idx = arr[right]
                cnt[idx] += 1
                if cnt[idx] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break
        return ans

This is slightly faster. But memory small. Could include. Need maybe if k=1, arr still built O(n). Fine. Could special-case k==1 before arr? Not necessary. But maybe if k=1, building arr and loops okay. Simpler to use ord inside. Which is more readable? Both.

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if k == 1:
            return n * (n + 1) // 2

        ans = 0
        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                idx = ord(s[right]) - 97
                cnt[idx] += 1
                if cnt[idx] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break
        return ans

This is robust. But if k=1, the general loop also works; special-case maybe more efficient and clear. However, if k=1, every substring valid; yes. Let's include? It adds a branch but okay. The problem constraints k>=1. Good.

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if k == 1:
            return n * (n + 1) // 2

        arr = [ord(c) - 97 for c in s]
        ans = 0
        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                idx = arr[right]
                cnt[idx] += 1
                if cnt[idx] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break
        return ans

```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if k == 1:
            return n * (n + 1) // 2

        arr = [ord(c) - 97 for c in s]
        ans = 0

        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                idx = arr[right]
                cnt[idx] += 1
                if cnt[idx] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break

        return ans
```

Let's maybe think if there is a possible issue with "n - k + 1" if k=1 and no special-case? no.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfSubstrings(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if k == 1:
            return n * (n + 1) // 2

        arr = [ord(c) - 97 for c in s]
        ans = 0

        for left in range(n - k + 1):
            cnt = [0] * 26
            for right in range(left, n):
                idx = arr[right]
                cnt[idx] += 1
                if cnt[idx] >= k:
                    ans += n - right
                    break

        return ans
```
